#6917. The Kitchen Trends Designers Expect to See Everywhere in 2027

The National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 forecast has arrived.

Above: the kitchen of an Athens home designed by Nashmita Rajiah.

Partial walls, hidden appliances, and pantries with room for prep are among the kitchen features expected to gain ground next year, according to the National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 Kitchen Trends Report. Based on a survey of 830 kitchen-industry professionals—including designers, manufacturers, remodelers, and architects—the report looks at the layouts, materials, lighting, and technology they expect to be popular over the next three years. Here are the main takeaways.

NKBA reports growing interest in partial walls, movable partitions, and separate kitchen zones—a shift from last year’s forecast, when open layouts were still gaining ground. Dedicated prep spaces and back kitchens are also emerging features, cited by nearly half of designers.

Kitchens are still expected to get bigger, with 52 percent of designers anticipating growth. The kitchen’s surrounding rooms are growing, too. NKBA reports that mudrooms and laundry spaces are increasingly being integrated into kitchen plans. The vast majority of designers expect attached mudrooms to be popular over the next three years, with 45 percent anticipating mudrooms getting larger and 43 percent expecting laundry spaces to grow.

Appliances are increasingly being incorporated into cabinetry rather than left on display. Eighty-five percent of designers agree cabinets are being designed to hide appliances and other functional elements, and roughly two-thirds say open shelving is on its way out in favor of enclosed storage.

Pantries are taking on more work, too. Nearly 90 percent agree they are becoming multifunctional extensions of the kitchen, and three-quarters expect dedicated small-appliance stations to be popular. Separate beverage areas continue to gain support, with 90 percent anticipating their popularity, up from 85 percent in the 2026 report.

Neutrals remain the leading color of choice, followed by greens and blues. For cabinetry, light and medium tones draw equal interest, and white oak is the preferred wood for roughly half of designers. Islands don’t necessarily have to match: a little more than half expect them to feature a different color from the surrounding cabinets. Quartzite and quartz lead countertop preferences, with nearly half of designers favoring honed or matte finishes.

The lighting forecast includes under-cabinet lights, illuminated cabinet interiors, nighttime path lighting, sconces, and pendants. Eighty-four percent of designers agree kitchens will incorporate multiple layers of light, and 78 percent say lighting is increasingly being used as a design feature rather than just a functional necessity. Under-cabinet lighting leads the forecast at 87 percent, followed by interior-cabinet lighting at 76 percent.

NKBA identifies ventilation, water filtration, touchless faucets, and sound-absorbing materials among the report’s wellness features. Designers are also discussing ways to make kitchens more resilient: 65 percent cite leak-detection systems and automatic shut-off valves, 62 percent cite backup power, and a little over half point to ventilation and air filtration.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6915. Everything We Know About ‘Stranger Things’ Prequel, ‘Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure’

We might have said goodbye to Hawkins, but that doesn’t mean we know everything when it comes to the Upside Down. On Stranger Things Day aka November 6, Netflix dropped a big surprise when they announced a brand-new spin-off series, Other Worlds. Only this time we’re not going to be heading back to Indiana, nor are we seeing anything that happened after the series ended. Instead, the brand-new sequel is actually a prequel and we’re going to be uncovering way more than we ever imagined. Ready to find out more?

Here’s everything you need to know about Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure.

Yes, Netflix revealed on November 6, 2026—also known as Stranger Things Day—that a brand new prequel series, Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure, was in the works on the streamer.

Like most things in the Stranger Things universe, Netflix is keeping a lot of the details under wraps. However, we did manage to get some major clues in the teaser and initial announcement that they sent out to press.

The series will go back in time—April 18, 1955, to be exact—and several states away from Hawkins, Indiana, to Marion, Nevada. Bigger details are still being kept a secret, but since it goes back even before Stranger Things: The First Shadow, which showed the origin of Henry Creel/Vecna, we’re probably going to learn a lot more about the Upside Down’s beginnings.

In a press release co-creators/executive producers Matt and Ross Duffer said, “We can’t wait for you to see what they’re cooking up. It’s Stranger Things, but also thrillingly fresh, with a new setting, new heroes and villains, and new… actually, we’re not allowed to say any more.”

Casting hasn’t been announced and neither have the characters. In the initial press release sent over, the Duffer Brothers noted that it was next in their process, so we’ll definitely be hearing some more news soon!

No official release date has been announced just yet, but it was revealed that filming will be starting up in New Mexico soon and that the series is slated to premiere in 2028.

#6909. Megan Skiendel of KATSEYE Reveals the Real Reason for the Recent String of Concert Cancellations

The group postponed three shows after previously citing Megan’s illness.

It’s no secret that KATSEYE’s Wildworld Tour has been...a bit rocky, to say the least. With two of its members—Sophia Laforteza and Manon Bannerman—taking extended breaks from performing with the global girl group, the four remaining members (Daniela Avanzini, Lara Raj, Megan Skiendel, and Yoonchae) kicked off the tour’s North American leg on October 13, only to end up canceling their shows in Detroit, Minneapolis, and—most recently—Chicago.

The first cancellation occurred on November 3, less than an hour before the group was set to perform at Little Caesars Arena in Detroit. At the time, the group released an Instagram Story apology, saying the show was “postponed due to illness” and that “Megan is unwell and unable to perform.”

Two days later, on November 5, the official Instagram pages for Chicago’s United Center and Minneapolis’ Target Center shared statements announcing the postponements, once again citing Megan’s illness.

Not long after, the official KATSEYE Instagram account released another Story statement, this time explaining what was really going on.

“The label has made the decision to postpone our shows in Chicago and Minneapolis as Megan is recovering from a concussion,” the group wrote, adding that they would “announce the rescheduled dates as soon as possible.”

Soon after, Megan took to her own Stories to release a statement, telling fans, “I currently have a concussion and am doing everything I possibly can to recover from it so I can be on stage for you all again.”

She added that she’s been “strongly advised by medical professionals that I need to take this time to fully recover and come back safely.”

Quick reminder that this ^^^ all comes after KATSEYE was forced to pull out of Olivia Rodrigo’s Daisy Chain Fields festival back in August after Megan sprained her ankle during tour rehearsal. Per the band, Megan was evaluated by doctors and told to stay off her ankle and allow it to properly heal ahead of the group’s upcoming tour.

All this to say: Can we all agree that these women need (and deserve) an extended break??

#6908. On the Couch with Christopher John Rogers

In honor of his new collaboration with The Rug Company, the fashion designer shares his inspirations and style icons.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Christopher John Rogers has an eye for color. The fashion designer, born in Baton Rouge and based in New York, established his self-titled label in 2016. Over the past decade, he’s become known for his use of bold tones and voluminous silhouettes, and for dressing the likes of Michelle Obama and Cardi B. Now, Rogers is taking his style to the floor in a new collaboration with The Rug Company.

The collection, which Rogers designed with Will Cooper, creative director of The Rug Company, feels alive, with vibrant, swooshing marks, multicolored doodles in wool and silk, and wavy threads that appear to dance across the floor. “The scribbles, the brushstrokes, the happy accidents, the lines that don't quite meet,” Rogers tells ELLE Decor. “I love the idea of taking something spontaneous and seemingly incidental and rendering it permanent through this incredibly meticulous, centuries-old craft.”

Launching tomorrow, the collaboration includes six styles across ten colorways. But Rogers has more planned beyond womenswear, “exploring new ways for the brand to exist beyond the wardrobe,” he says. “I'm particularly excited by opportunities that allow us to think about color, proportion, texture, and materiality through different disciplines, whether that's furniture, interiors, or something entirely unexpected.”

Below, Rogers takes ELLE Decor’s On the Couch survey, sharing a few of his favorite pieces, destinations, and entertaining takes.

Favorite Sofa: Pierre Paulin's ABCD sofa for Artifort. The color theory is delicious, and I'd happily sacrifice a little comfort for something that fabulous.

Architect You Admire: Ettore Sottsass. I love the clarity of his vision and his ability to make the simplest shapes feel monumental.

Destination Museum: The Louisiana Museum of Modern Art in Denmark

Go-to Galleries: 52 Walker, Broadway Gallery, and Salon 94 in New York.

Favorite Artist/Artwork: I've recently fallen for Domenica Brockman. We have one of her paintings in the studio, and I'm constantly struck by how much emotion she can communicate through deceptively simple arrangements of color.

Vintage Piece of Your Dreams: An original Verner Panton Living Tower.

Hotels to Check Into: Hotel Saint Vincent in New Orleans, and La Mamounia in Marrakech.

Entertaining Tips: Great lighting, a boozy cocktail, and a playlist with a point of view. Don't overthink it!

Style Icons: Bert and Ernie, and Isaac Mizrahi.

Required Reading: Interaction of Color by Josef Albers and The Bluest Eye by Toni Morrison. Two very different but equally essential ways of learning to see.

Cocktail or Mocktail of Choice: It's a toss-up between a wet Roku martini and a Cynar spritz.

Favorite Restaurant: Cervo’s in the Lower East Side, Rolo's in Ridgewood, Frenchette in Tribeca, and Santa Panza in Bushwick.

Favorite Jeweler: Presley Oldham and Bernard James for contemporary jewelry, and vintage Alexander Calder or Alber Elbaz-era Lanvin for something a little more fantastical.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6906. I Found the Stocking Stuffers Your Beauty-Obsessed Friend Will Actually Use

Including easy-to-divide gift sets for extra savings.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Big-ticket beauty gifts like a Dyson hair tool or an LED mask may get all the attention during the holidays, but some of the most coveted presents come in small packages—aka stocking stuffers. As a beauty editor, my favorite gifts to give (and receive...hint, hint) are the little treasures I can toss in my purse and reach for all day. Consider this your expert guide to the best beauty stocking stuffers to stock up on this holiday season.

Think of this list as a mini beauty haul, with plenty of editor-backed goodies they’ll actually use, from pimple patch restocks and luxurious hand creams to travel-friendly picks that can make seat 34F feel like first class. Pro tip: I also included gift sets you can split up to fill multiple stockings and save a few bucks. Most picks are under $50, but I also threw in a few luxe finds for anyone you’re ready to spoil.

Medicube’s latest viral hit is basically made for a stocking. This easy-to-swipe balm stick pairs PDRN, collagen, caffeine, vitamin E, and Volufiline to hydrate and give skin a plumper-looking finish wherever they want it, from under the eyes to smile lines. It’s a fun upgrade to their usual eye patch routine—and one they can use on the go.

Our editors—along with the rest of the internet—got hooked on Eos’ Vanilla Cashmere line. The Shea Better body lotion gives winter skin a serious hit of hydration without feeling heavy, and the scent is so good. While the classic body lotion is always an old faithful, why not gift them something with a little extra glam like this holiday edition shimmery version? It’s the same nourishing formula, but with a golden, pearlescent finish for glowy skin.

Another useful beauty stocking stuffer? Dae’s mascara-inspired finishing wand will tame flyaways and frizz for a polished look. The styling cream gives hair flexible hold without leaving it crunchy or stiff. Plus, it’s convenient to carry in a handbag so they can touch up whenever, wherever.

I’ll never say no to more hydrating lippies—and the scents on my personal wish list? Summer Fridays’ vanilla beige, hot cocoa, and brown sugar. Formulated with shea butter, murumuru seed butter, and hyaluronic acid, this viral lip balm leaves a glassy finish while imparting a buildable tint. Plus, we’ve found that despite its compact size, it lasts for months.

These hydrogel masks are one of the most viral products for achieving an overnight glow—and our editors can confirm they’re worth the obsession. You can never have too many claw clips—so they’re sure to appreciate this stylish four-pack that comes in soothing colors and bedazzled designs. They are available in a range of colors and patterns, so pick a few that match their aesthetic.

If they’rea already a fan of Charlotte Tilbury’s Pillow Talk line, give them this Plumpgasm lip gloss. #6903. Under the Surface

Canali gives a behind-the-scenes look at sartorial excellence: the time, precision and mastery that transform raw materials into a jacket made to last

Under the surface, unseen layers lie. They stay there, silent. They don’t demand the spotlight. They don’t seek glory. And yet, like a beating heart, they form the innermost foundation of any real achievement. Where passion, enthusiasm, and discipline meet, the logic of excellence takes shape. Such is the story of Under the Surface, the newest creative collaboration from Canali.

Under the Surface is a look at the unseen architecture of a sartorial jacket by Canali. Every Canali jacket is born from a complex system of about 200 steps, each executed with meticulous care. Around 15 specialists are involved in the process—one that sees more than 520 feet of thread sewn into 22,000 stitches, both machine- and hand-made. Unseen when looking at the finished garment, the process informs each piece’s quality, comfort, shape, and durability.

It’s not just a matter of talent: rather, it’s about staying faithful to one’s goals, pursuing them with dedication even when no one is watching. It’s then that quality truly makes a difference, and the end result becomes the visible manifestation of a long creation process that demands great effort, experience and willpower.

That commitment of excellence unites the sartorial world of Canali and Ethan Hawke, the brand’s new Global Ambassador. The actor, writer, and director has dedicated almost four decades to perfecting his art, with endless, ever-learning curiosity. For their new Fall/Winter 2026 campaign, Canali has decided to capture what goes on behind the scenes. In the film, Hawke pores over his scripts, rehearses lines, refines each motion: a painstaking introspective process made of research, study, and exercise, preceding each of his performances.

The behind-the-scenes of a Canali jacket starts with selecting the finest fabrics, sourced from the best Italian wool mills. Then, each textile is examined and interpreted. While technology now allows extreme precision and control, some assessments are still left to the eye and expertise of human specialists. Even tiny details such as slightly different shades of color or variations in texture can influence the final product.

The fabric is then stabilized via steaming cycles and specific treatments, to ensure its behavior remains consistent over time. Then it’s time for digital mapping, guiding every cutting room decision: here, each of the jacket’s pieces is carefully positioned to also help keep waste to a minimum. It’s the first irreversible step.

The garment’s three-dimensional structure is then shaped upon the canvas. Canali crafts theirs in-house, adapting it to each model’s fit and fabrics. Made from horsehair, plant fibers, and natural reinforcements, the canvas is modeled on custom-made concave shapes, known as Schiena d’Asino (Donkey’s Back), which replicate the chest’s anatomy. The canvas is crucial for determining how a jacket drapes over the body, avoiding stiffness. It’s here that the volume of the chest, the balance of the silhouette and ease of wearing begin to be defined.

Then it’s time to craft the front, lapel, padded shoulder, and the sleeves. From planning to execution, nothing’s left to chance. The tailor observes, corrects, iterates. It’s a patient process, requiring time, knowledge, and skill. Lastly, the various layers are united through basting, stitching, and ironing processes. As these details interact, the garment takes on its structure and sophisticated character.

Something special also takes place behind the scenes with Hawke: day after day, the actor’s preparation subtly transforms his thoughts and behavior until, almost by instinct, he takes on each role’s intimate characteristics.

Time underpins the whole process. After being assembled and pressed, the jacket is allowed to rest, to dissipate the steam and stabilize the cloth. This is how the fabric finds its balance, and the garment gains a sort of memory: the ability to naturally find its shape again, even after having been worn, folded, or packed into a suitcase.

In over 90 years of heritage, Canali has refined their techniques through repetition, experience, and experimentation. Each garment is built upon the time-tested values of care, know-how, precision. The Canali style is not one made of grand gestures, but daily practice, intimate and subtle: the constant research into a shape that lasts. One hero of the Under the Surface project is the vicuña jacket, available exclusively at selected flagship boutiques by Canali.The campaign, and the collaboration between Canali and Ethan Hawke, speak to the art of shaping oneself, guided by an ambition to do things well with patience and self-awareness. Both in tailoring and in cinema, there is so much quiet work that happens behind the scenes to make creativity possible. Discipline and rigour do not constrain creative freedom; they make it possible. For Canali, excellence takes shape through the craftsmanship with which each element is made. For Ethan Hawke, it is the preparation that allows him to approach each role with confidence.And, as the Under The Surface campaign reveals, each of these steps adds something essential to the finished product. In the case of the Canali cashmere and vicuña blazer (the hero style of the FW26 campaign), it is structure, movement, character, durability, and a lightness that can only be achieved through the combination of expertly-chosen materials and complex fine tailoring. The knowledge required to execute this is almost tangible, felt in the quality of the piece.

The finished result only has value because of the strong, unseen structure underneath. It is a story we can all resonate with. Little by little, layer by layer, day by day, we each build ourselves in pursuit of excellence. Our actions create the people we are – even, or especially, when nobody is watching – in the same way that the apparent simplicity of a piece of clothing hides the depth of work required to craft it. This is the essence of Under the Surface.

#6900. After the Midterms, Millennial Optimism Makes Sense Again

None of us are the same people we were in 2008. But it’s okay, just for a day, to bask in hope.

I have told this story many times in writing, but in 2008 I was so hopeful on election night that I stripped off my clothes with a bar full of people, right before they called Pennsylvania for Obama, to take a picture and commemorate the moment. They took the photo. We got up off the sticky bar floor, pulled our clothes back on, and literally danced in the streets with the cops while the Obama reggaeton song played. This was peak millennial political optimism, and the rush of it could make you believe that all the contradictions of this country didn’t matter. That we were all moving forward together.

In 2008, I was a junior researcher at a Black history museum, a place where people thought deeply about legacy and historical sea change. I remember being so deeply excited, after seven years of political insanity post-9/11, that maybe the United States was finally waking up. It was a shock, then, to talk to my older coworkers, most of whom had been involved with or studied the Black radical tradition, and hear their cautious indifference to what felt like a coming revolution. “We’ll see,” most of them said—a pragmatism about this country that I didn’t fully understand back then. Back then I hoped, deep down, that what those older coworkers were experiencing was merely sour grapes.

In 2026, of course, we are all living through the political backlash that they were sure was coming and the missteps of moderation during the Obama years that set the stage for Trump. I don’t really hope about politics anymore. Yes, I know hope is a discipline. Hope is something that, by definition, you have to have when things are dire. But the way hope lives in my body—a frantic, desperate thing that plummets when it meets catastrophe—means national politics aren’t a good place for that emotion. When I talk about politics now with friends, it’s mostly to see who can make the darkest joke about our current timeline.

But after last night, things are different. Like a lot of people, I’m allowing myself one calendar day to actually feel excited about the state of the world. I’m still saying “Let’s see,” but for right now, every time someone tries to bring up why we’re still doomed, I keep scrolling. I can do bad all by myself tomorrow.

Since Trump entered the scene 10 years ago, there’s been an active disdain for sincerity in politics. The reasons for this are many. Trump and MAGA ran on the belief that becoming great could only be measured by how badly the people you hated were suffering. Those who would oppose Trump grew cynical about the national Democratic party’s dance of fearmongering about the state of the country and steadfastly refusing to do anything meaningful about it when given the power to do so. Between these two extremes, it felt foolish to care. It felt smarter and safer to view politics the way a consultant or a pundit might, with the distance of someone who keeps telling you he has no skin in the game. As if that position was a superior one. As if that distance could save you any heartbreak or keep the threat of a layoff or a medical bill or an eviction at bay.

To care is to be cringe, and in the last few years, being cringe has been one of the greatest cultural sins, a devastating social faux pas. But last night was such a seismic shift in American politics that the idea of cringe is currently blissfully irrelevant. Being cringe enough to care brought millions to the polls and a historic midterm turnout. Being cringe enough to care meant that people like James Talarico, Abdul El-Sayed, and Angie Nixon launched campaigns even as everyone told their potential base that voting was irrelevant, that nothing could ever change about their states.

I’m not the same person I was in 2008; hopefully none of us are. And this is not the same country. But to make sure this country is one where I want to be present, I have to believe on some level that it’s possible. To change anything in this world, you first have to care enough to want to do it.

#6898. When to Arrive at (and Leave) a Party, According to the Pros

What to know about being on the fashionable side of fashionably late.

Once again, we find ourselves on the verge of party season, that most wonderful (and exhausting) time of year. The nights are long and busy; the days are short and tired—and it’s all totally worth it. This season also ushers in the usual quandaries: What to bring, what to wear, and, perhaps the most confusing of all, when to arrive.

Everyone agrees that the best dinner party guests know to give their hosts a bit of time past the official start, but how much is right? And how does that change for a cocktail party? Fear not; we’ve gathered the best and brightest party-goers to answer all those questions: how to be late enough to be fashionable and early enough to be polite, courtesy of those who know best.

ELLE Decor Rising Star Romilly Newman, the chef and food stylist who towers her chocolate cakes as high as her well-decked greenery, thinks “30 minutes is the sweet spot,” she says. “As a frequent host, I'm always running behind and secretly wishing everyone would arrive late. Whether it's running to buy bags of ice, ironing the tablecloth, or an extra braise in the oven.” She warns, however, “An hour late is when it starts to get into dangerous territory—as the host is most likely waiting to serve a hot meal.” But, when it comes to dinner, when it’s time to sit down, it’s also time to stay down: “Cocktail parties are come and go, dinner parties are a nightlong commitment.”

Ivy Getty, a stalwart of guest lists from Milan to Manhattan, suggests arriving a bit earlier: “The best window is 15-20 minutes, so as not to rush the host. I don’t really think of these as rules: they feel like common sense.” She adds: “Obviously, if you are going to a funeral or graduation you arrive early—or on time at the latest.”

Ryan Nordheimer, a New Yorker who plans elaborate monthly party potlucks, suggests that “the sweet spot” for those headed to dinner parties is 5 to 15 minutes after the host tells you to arrive. He adds, “please never be early. Those 5 extra minutes always come in handy as the host.” If it’s a cocktail party, however, particularly one on the longer side, “you can arrive right on time to 30 minutes late.” Plus, he says, “You can always text the host the day before and ask—just don’t ask them the night of.”

Then, when every interesting topic has been exhausted, and any talk about the weather has completely dried up, there’s the question of departures. Newman says, “I like to think of a dinner party like a play in three acts. There's the mingling, the meal, and the lingering. When the host starts clearing the table and blowing out candles, it's time to pack up.”

Nordheimer has a more scientific approach to closing time: on “a weeknight, I like to have people leave around 10pm,” he says. He’ll go an hour later on weekends. “But every host is different. The host reserves the right to kick people out; just start yawning a lot to make it obvious,” he notes. Clear signals—and choosing guests who can pick up on them—is key.

As for how a host should plan, “You should have most of your food done by the time guests arrive,” says Newman. At the end of the day, though, as long as a guest arrives ready to keep the energy high and be gracious to their host, they can’t go wrong.

Dorothy Scarborough (she/her) is the assistant to the Editor in Chief of Town & Country and Elle Decor.

#6897. Lean Into Chocolate Brown Coats to Look Sumptuous as the Weather Drops

It’s the color of the year, at this point!

It’s been made very clear that chocolate brown is the color of the fall and winter at this point. It’s become a staple I cannot get away from, and I honestly don’t want to. So as we gear up for the remainder of fall, and start preparing for the chilly winter months, I’m tapping into the chocolate brown trend with my outerwear pieces, too.

I’m always on the hunt for a good trench coat, fur jacket, and everything in between once the temperature drops below 50 degrees. So if that’s you too, I’ve got some major style inspiration below for your perusing. Let’s get those winter closets ready because I don’t believe in sacrificing style for warmth.

A quality coat works for an everyday fall look, and this chocolate brown coloring adds depth to what is a simple outfit. Throw one on with your jeans and white top and you’re good to go all day.

Personally, the crewneck sweater is a very underutilized piece in everyone’s closet. It works so well as a layering top, especially during the fall months. Adding this chocolate brown one on top of a checkered button-down top is incredibly stylish and perfect for the season, especially when topped with a textured stole for added warmth.

It’s easy to rely on a black leather jacket because it’s what’s popular, but may I introduce you to the elevated, cooler cousin: a brown leather jacket. It softens your look and brings something different to the table when mapping out your fall outfits.

Whether you’re going to work or just really into business casual, a chocolate brown blazer is such a perfect addition to your closet. It’s polished, but also just a good outerwear staple to have on deck.

I am a huge fan of faux fur coats. They add so much drama to any look and why not tap into that chicness with a brown version? It’s trendy, but can also last for several seasons, never feeling out of style.

#6895. Trump’s White House Ballroom Reaches a Major Construction Milestone

The president signed the final steel beam for the 90,000-square-foot structure, which is slated to seat about 1,000 guests beneath 40-foot ceilings.

Construction on President Donald Trump’s White House ballroom continues to progress in the wake of the Supreme Court’s August ruling that allowed the once-stalled project to proceed. The structure has now officially topped out, meaning it’s reached its final height. On Wednesday, the day after the midterm elections, Trump invited reporters inside the 90,000-square-foot building as he signed the final structural steel beam before workers hoisted it into place.

The $600 million project, which sits on the site of the now-demolished East Wing, has a height of 51 feet and will include two above-ground floors, plus a new underground national security center. The Executive Residence portion of the White House reaches a height of 70 feet at its tallest point, the South Portico, although the majority of the structure is shorter.

Architect Shalom Baranes’s design closely aligns with the White House’s neoclassical style, with traditional colonnades, arches, and cornice lines. The centerpiece will be an elaborate 22,000-square-foot ballroom with a seating capacity of about 1,000. Trump has repeatedly claimed that the lavishly gilded space is set to have ceiling heights that reach 40 feet. Additionally, he revealed on Wednesday that marble for the floors had been selected and ordered.

The building is slated to hold office space for the First Lady, who traditionally kept an office in the East Wing, as well as a new movie theater to replace the one formerly located in the now-razed East Wing Colonnade.

Since taking office, Trump has proposed myriad construction projects in the nation’s capital, from a 250-foot-tall triumphal arch across the Potomac River from the Lincoln Memorial to a helipad on the South Lawn of the White House. The ballroom is expected to reach completion prior to the end of Trump’s term in January 2029.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6890. An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar UK. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Soft Services Theraplush Overnight Repair Treatment

Dior Huile Abricot Nutritive Serum for Nails and Cuticles

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6889. An Everyman’s Guide to Wearing a Necklace

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You already have a watch you like. This is the next step in your jewelry journey.

Men’s jewelry, I’ve learned, can be a slippery slope. I’ve been a watch guy since elementary school, if we’re generous enough to count a dual-sided compass/watch on a carabiner as a watch. In college I added a bracelet. I’ve been a ring guy for over ten years, thanks mostly to being married and partially to getting lunch drunk in Albuquerque, going ring shopping for my wife, and finding a sterling silver bear signet ring that I’ve worn nonstop since. I’ve had an earring for two years, thanks to a quick weekend in Miami and a well-timed Harrison Ford interview clip crossing my algorithm about getting his ear pierced in a Claire’s.

Recently, I’ve been curious about my personal final frontier of men’s jewelry—the necklace. The other listings above are all fairly low entry points, safe experiments in the world of men’s jewelry. The earring may be the exception, but this is 2026; one small silver hoop isn’t that daring, even in the Midwest. A necklace has never really been my thing. I haven’t felt the pull. That is, until this year.

For an unrelated project, I was breaking down some of the signature stylistic calling cards of Robert Redford. And there it was, across paparazzi pictures and movie stills and in memoriam roundups. In almost every photo, Redford has some kind of necklace, usually peeking from a precariously unbuttoned western shirt.

Further study of what we can safely call Hollywood’s golden age of off-duty style shows the same: McQueen and Newman and their contemporaries, swanning around on and off film, always wearing some sort of necklace or chain or pendant.

This is, of course, a common trap: to look at a famously handsome Hollywood star and think, yeah, all that’s separating me from THAT is a necklace. And sure, a good cheekbone and clavicle will get you pretty far in life. There’s no magic amulet here, nothing that will shazam you into leading-man looks. But there are a few lessons on styling from guys whose literal job it was to present themselves favorably to the world. And a few lessons on what necklaces to choose to help get there.

So, I put my professional online shopping style writing gig into overdrive, going deep into the Getty Images archives and confusing Google’s settings by repeatedly searching “Insert Hollywood Icon + shirtless” to glean some jewelry 101 tips from the professionals.

Instagram and (theoretically, since I don’t indulge) TikTok are full of maximalizing style trend chasers. Why wear one necklace when you can wear four? Why two rings when each finger could have two rings on it? Why dip your toes in the water when you can cannonball into the deep end? And sure, there’s some je ne sais quoi about just committing to something fully and completely out of the gate. But there’s also a lot of try-hard to it.

We’re channeling a more reserved, less #sponsoredpost era of dressing here that cares less about link clicks and more about how things look in the real world. So start simple and start with a chain. My favorite, and currently on daily rotation, is the Curb Chain from L.A.-based Bloedstone. Mine is sterling silver and in the inch length, which hits closer to the sternum than the neck. That’s the prescribed Hollywood A-lister way to wear it, but chart your own path if you want to. Miansai has a thicker 2mm Cuban chain that’s great too. And while we’re covering the M’s, Mejuri has one too. To my slight surprise, my home state watchmaker Shinola makes one, and less surprisingly so, Esquire-approved David Yurman has this giant one featuring a marketing campaign with Michael B. Jordan, who is picking up McQueen’s (and subsequently Brosnan’s) torch by adapting The Thomas Crown Affair.

There’s a whole vocabulary on link types and thickness and clasps out there. Don’t worry about that for now. Find something that calls to you and click add to cart. We’re starting slowly here.

Parting note. There are a lot of chains worn over T-shirts these days. To each their own, but the leading man’s move is to keep it under your shirt. If you want to show it off, just ditch the buttons.

A chain is simple, and sure, the greats wore them. But more often than not, they had something dangling from that chain. Medallion, pendant, a bottle opener in Cool Hand Luke. McQueen was often photographed, including in the pages of Esquire, wearing a St. Christopher medallion. Seeing as he’s the patron saint of travelers, St. Christopher medallions are having a bit of a cultural resurgence, partially because of their traditional association as a good-luck charm for travelers. There are some fun enamel ones on eBay, including these surfing-specific ones. Every once in a while, Nashville-based Americana experts Imogene + Willie will pull together a vintage edit of St. Christopher medallions that always sell out in around 24 hours. Speaking of Nashville, if you’re going to go new, go with Yearly Co. The gold jewelry experts have a 14k version of the iconography with a pitch-perfect 24-inch chain. I can attest to the quality—I gave my wife the brand’s diamond huggies and she wears them almost every day. As the grandson of a Protestant minister, I steer away from the saints out of genetic loyalty to Luther’s 95 Theses, but I recently picked up this Perseverance medallion from the brand, inspired by a vintage signet ring. It scratches the McQueen itch with flying colors.

Secondhand gold is also an option. Alloy Market recently came onto my radar, and I’m browsing it daily for myself and gifts for my wife. They’ve got a small but mighty collection of certified men’s items with pendants and medallions you can mix and match. All of the quality without any of the markup.

Don’t limit yourself to just a medallion, though. Get a little funky. Redford did for a while with this turquoise number that somehow looked even cooler hitting under the sternum. Shinola’s got an option there too. Bali-based Salty Dagger has an Odysseus-approved Greek mythology piece. And why not try a locket, like this one from Pattaraphan. Miansai has a great pendant. Get fancy with the Prevaut diamond ring deal. When in doubt, steal from the girls. I got this With Clarity birthstone necklace for my wife that I might take a swing at myself.

Peyote Bird High Desert Sterling Silver Necklace

Shinola 21mm Turquoise Inlay Tag Necklace

From the abbreviated/slightly bastardized Shakespeare: “All the world’s a stage and one man in his time plays many parts.” Jewelry can feel like a risk, even if you’re following some sort of contrived guide to styling and buying it. Sometimes we pretend until we’re comfortable actually being. Redford and McQueen and company were actors. Pretending was their legacy, and they were good at it. Take a leaf from their book and get dressed for the curtain call.

#6887. 5 Fall Trends Designers Think Will Still Look Good Next Spring

Above: A hallway view of Eric Egan’s Milan apartment.

If any season has the power to inspire a complete aesthetic overhaul, it’s fall. Nature’s changing palette inspires us to follow suit, as each year our mood boards make new space for jewel tones, cozy textures, and a bottomless candle budget. Autumnal trends are so alluring, it almost feels like a risky time to make long-term design decisions. But with the right consideration, that seasonal inspiration can translate into choices that look good year-round.

This fall in particular, designers are urging clients to trust their guts—making sure they see themselves in the spaces they create, rather than blindly following the latest fads. “For me, a trend has to be authentic or it’s just noise,” says designer Chad Dorsey, who’s eager to ditch the spare minimalism of years past for looks with more personality. ELLE Decor A-Lister Kerry Joyce seconds the motion: “By prioritizing personalized storytelling, a space becomes evergreen, evolving as smaller objects are swapped out over time.”

But an expert nudge in the right direction never hurts. This fall, top designers are gravitating toward creative color-drenching, cleverly concealed storage, tactile upholstery, and rooms built around art. Read on for their takes on the fall 2026 trends worth investing in, plus tips on building (as Dorsey puts it) “spaces designed to be lived in, not looked at.”

Fabric and upholstery are trending ultra plush and textured for fall, but when thoughtfully integrated, they feel more timeless than seasonal. “Design is shifting toward how a space feels, not just how it looks,” says Joyce, who’s seeing an increased popularity in fabrics like mohair, velvet, cashmere, and alpaca hair. “This focus on texture does more than elevate a room's aesthetic,” he explains. “It creates a sensory experience that comforts the soul.”

Start your bedroom redesign this fall and your space will strike the perfect balance between fresh and lived-in by spring. Between client requests and a personal home build, Amber Lewis has bedrooms on the brain right now. One trend takes the lead in her eyes: “I love matching the walls to the bed upholstery and bedding so the whole room feels wrapped in one warm color family. It’s calm and peaceful, and that enveloping feeling works in any season.”

Luckily, this cozy trend doesn’t require a complete room overhaul: “The easiest place to start is the bed itself. Reupholster your headboard in a pattern you love, or drape a vintage suzani across the foot of the bed,” Lewis says. “One move like that changes the whole mood of the room without starting over.” To bring in a little dimension and pop, she recommends adding a singular statement, like a lone striped pillow.

If your primary suite needs a more holistic update, Lewis recommends creating a small sitting area. Sourcing vintage chairs and small sofas for primary suites this fall has given Lewis a new perspective on where bedroom design is headed: “True sanctuaries that feel layered, personal, and built for actually living in.”

Color drenching isn’t going anywhere for fall 2026—or long after, according to Dorsey. When choosing a shade for a room, he finds that any color works harder when it’s layered and intentional. Color capping—a term coined by Benjamin Moore referring to painting a room in multiple tones from one color family—can help keep a space from feeling flat or overwhelmed beneath a statement hue.

According to Dorsey, the staying power of color drenching goes hand in hand with the shift away from spareness and toward “collected” spaces: “Neither is really a ‘look;’ they’re about living with things you love, which is why they’ll carry well into spring.”

Designer Regan Baker says that cleverly hidden space solutions are also on the rise. “It could be a TV that slides behind a commissioned art panel, a Murphy bed tucked into a wall of millwork or that serves as a desk when it's stored, or a peg wall that’s a place to hang a jacket one minute and a standing desk the next,” she says. These solutions are tailored to her clients’ spaces (rather than tied to a season or palette), which gives them staying power.

This concept is an easy way to turn the corner you’ve never known how to style into your favorite part of the room. “In an existing home, look at the awkward spots, like an alcove or the wall around a fireplace, and treat built-ins there as a design moment instead of an afterthought,” Baker advises.

If there’s one built-to-last fall trend most designers can agree on, it’s added eccentricity. “Homes are steering away from the sterile, overnight look of a showroom delivery,” Joyce says. “The defining philosophy now focuses on layering modern staples with antiques, secondhand finds, travel mementos, and new pieces that carry a sense of patina.”

Dorsey describes this style as “collected rather than prescribed,” where a high-low mix of vintage and custom elements gel together. And while it may feel loftier than a trending, sold-out piece, Baker says that most clients start with an artistic anchor, then build around it: “We’re designing more homes around a piece of art than around a color trend. That might be wallpaper by local designers, a commissioned piece, or even a palette pulled from a lichen-covered rock found on the property.”

One key takeaway? Curate like a collector. Your reward may be a space that never goes out of style.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6886. I Tried Hailey Bieber’s Favorite Creatine Gummies for Two Months, and I Have Thoughts

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why these candylike gumdrops have converted me into a creatine lover

The supplement landscape has never been murkier, more confusing, or more overwhelming. While I have a pretty extensive wellness regimen, it feels like there are a million and one powders, pills, and gummies I should be adding to my routine. One supplement that keeps coming into the spotlight is creatine.

I first heard about creatine from my 62-year-old mother, who waxed poetic about the supplement. Then, the influencer of all influencers, Hailey Bieber, posted on Instagram a few weeks later a creatine gummy from Arrae. I thought: That’s it. I’m taking the creatine plunge.

“Creatine works like an energy recycler,” says Roxanne Pero, a Dallas-based obstetrician and gynecologist and part of O Positiv’s Medical Advisory Board. “Every cell in the body needs energy, and that energy comes from a molecule called ATP, or adenosine triphosphate. Creatine helps the body regenerate ATP more quickly, especially when energy runs low, and especially in tissues that use a lot of it, like muscle and the brain.”

As someone who works out five times a week, often in heated classes that push my body to depletion, I figured I was the perfect candidate for creatine. But it’s actually one of those supplements anyone can benefit from; it kicks in at moments when our cells need more energy—not just during strenuous exercise, but also during sleep deprivation and stress, which everyone experiences at some point. It’s even recommended for perimenopause, menopause, and postpartum.

“Active women in their 20s and 30s may benefit from improved training performance, while women approaching menopause or experiencing it may be interested in supporting muscle strength as part of a broader strategy that includes resistance training and adequate protein,” says Rachel Brief, a registered dietitian and wellness advisor at the Lanby.

Recently, the supplement has gained traction among women in their 40s and above. “As a woman enters menopause and postmenopause, the decrease in estrogen contributes to age-related muscle and strength loss,” says Shilpa Raut, the vice president of R&D at Cymbiotika, who holds a master’s degree and a PhD in pharmaceutical sciences. Creatine can help women maintain muscle mass, support steady metabolic health, and improve cognitive resilience, which often declines with age.

If you’re wondering why everyone isn’t already taking this magic supplement, well, I was too. Creatine has long been associated with gym bros, and it has a bad reputation for causing bloating, which is not a risk I’m willing to take as a girl with a sensitive gut. I also wasn’t excited about consuming a chalky white powder. But Arrae’s Tone gummies tout all of the benefits without the bloat, and they come in a candylike form. “Creatine had always been marketed to men, because no one had ever presented it in a way that felt like it was made for women,” Arrae’s cofounder Siffat Haider tells me. “Our customers adopted it immediately, and that demand is why we kept building on the flavors.”

The one caveat with creatine is that you need to drink water to get the full benefits. “Creatine draws water into muscle cells, and that is part of how it works,” says Michael Fredericson, a sports-medicine physiatrist, professor at Stanford University, and Elysium Scientific Advisory Board member. If you’re dehydrated, creatine will pull your body’s existing water supply into your muscles, causing dehydration and hindering normal bodily functions.

I’m now two months into my experiment with Arrae Tone. Whenever I tell people that I started taking creatine, they ask me the same question: “Does it actually work?” My verdict: I don’t plan to stop taking it any time soon. Experts say creatine is especially beneficial as a supplement to strength-training workouts, so I was excited to see how it amplified my workouts. Even a few weeks into taking creatine, I noticed more definition in my abdominals and arms, areas where I’ve historically struggled to see muscle.

The Arrae gummies have no chalky taste and are easy to swallow. I take a lot of different powders and pills, so having a delicious gummy is a nice change of pace. Plus, the dosage is only 3.75 to 5 grams (depending on whether you take three or four gummies), which is on the lower end of the spectrum and has been proven to be effective without causing the dreaded bloating. And it’s creatine monohydrate, which is the gold standard of creatine, Brief explains. Still, they’re not my only form of creatine. On days when I’d rather add a powder to a smoothie or my water to boost hydration, I love the Elysium Health powders; they’re only 5 grams, so they’re easy on my stomach, and they come in delicious fruity flavors that taste like nostalgic lemonade.

#6882. Zillennials Don’t Feel the Need to Grow Up, and Club Kid Knows Why

Jordan Firstman’s deferred coming-of-age tale speaks to the realities of living in an unwieldy time

I’m 29, an age that has become a catalyst for conversations about what it means to be an adult, settling down (or not), and—perhaps above all—how to show up for my own well-being. (Do I need to party less?) It’s a volley of topics that are often the subject of yearly think pieces, even when we know the conclusions they’ll reach. Millennials and Zillennials feel pressured by older generations to live a conventional and ambitious life, even as they’ve squeezed young people’s hope for a future.

The New York Times detailed this dilemma just last month, speaking to 30-somethings across New York City who felt discouraged by their situation and, to put it plainly, left behind. The article pointed out that the long-running Harvard Youth Poll found that for the first time ever, most young Americans don’t believe they will surpass their parents financially. In the last 30 years, the cost of living has skyrocketed, and, today, mounting loans and debt among young people have made a house feel out of reach. Looking even younger, Gen Z is feeling the brunt of the economy, with Fortune finding that two thirds of parents say their Gen Z adult kids rely on them (and Playboy going so far as to connect the housing crisis to the sex decline). Why stop partying if the rent’s too high, if there’s no reasonable expectation that you’ll find security, buy a house, or live easily past 2050?

This growing frustration is likely why audiences—including myself—are connecting so deeply with influencer-turned-director Jordan Firstman’s sensational feature debut, Club Kid. The film offers a tender look at arrested development and unexpected fatherhood, but, above all, it delivers an incisive look at the struggle to let go of youth when the world feels increasingly grim. The idea of partying instead of worrying is alluring: to exist in a space free from responsibility. Peter (Firstman), a 30-something party promoter deeply enmeshed in New York’s underground queer club scene, lives in the apartment his late mother left him and sells drugs on the side, and his only real daily concern is recovering for the next party. The moment he crosses the threshold of any club, he plunges into a fugue state fueled by drugs and sex. Peter clings to his youth (his name aptly echoing Peter Pan syndrome), shirks all responsibilities, and actively defers a much-needed inward look.

Peter’s wake-up call arrives in the form of a 10-year-old boy named Arlo (Reggie Absolom)—a son he unknowingly fathered years after hooking up with a man who insisted they turn their dalliance into a threesome with a woman. A decade after that fateful night, the woman’s best friend (played with drug-fueled frenzy by Kirby Howell-Baptiste) shows up at his door, a young child in tow, with news that the mother has died by suicide and Peter needs to step up. It’s a parable tailor-made for a worn-down generation that sees no reason to stop the party. It takes a ketamine stork of sorts to change course.

Club Kid revels in the growing pains. Firstman avoids reducing the conflict to a simple clash over parenting Arlo or his personal struggle to break free from the party lifestyle; instead, Club Kid unfolds as a tender parallel coming-of-age story, where Peter grows up as he learns to parent Arlo, a quiet, curious kid grappling with the loss of his mother. Firstman embraces the missteps, letting us see the beauty of his life as it intermingles with his newfound parenting. The whirlwind of responsibility and unwitting fatherhood plays like a fairy tale—a wondrous reality that feels increasingly distant from what a 30-something can reasonably expect today.

Watching Club Kid, I considered my own parents, who conceived me (by happy accident, I’m told) at 27 and 29. They worked at a pizza restaurant before opening their own bar. My childhood felt wrapped up in Atlanta’s nightlife scene; somehow, completely inexperienced, they still learned to balance their lifestyle with their newfound roles as parents. I lived as the only kid (with my two-years-younger brother) in my parents’ world for several years, often sitting with the bar regulars for hours while my parents worked or being actively watched over by the cooks. It felt like tapping into another world, but it also brought us closer. They didn’t give up their life for me; they made me a part of it. These days, this story sounds like a romantic ideal, a whirlwind adventure only possible without looming loans and the high cost of living. I simply couldn’t imagine doing the same today.

What makes Peter’s story compelling is how he navigates the responsibilities of fatherhood without fully forgetting himself. Some critics have challenged the film, claiming that it’s stigmatizing the queer party scene, but in his telling, he doesn’t rebuke his former lifestyle but rather the parts of it that were bad for him. His friend even welcomes the news of his fatherhood with excitement, spending time with Arlo without passing judgment. Even if he eventually needs to distance himself from these friends’ party habits, it doesn’t need a blowout fight. Life happens, and people move on.

That’s why it’s important that Firstman returns to the real problem at the end of the movie. A vindictive call to social services by Peter’s ex-best friend (Cara Delevigne) leads to Arlo’s deportation and the new father-son duo’s separation. It brings to light the real-life anxieties looming over this heartwarming story. Sure, Peter did not file the proper adoption papers, and his lack of money becomes damningly apparent when he stares down health care premiums and legal bills in the face of an unrelenting bureaucracy.

Firstman refuses to provide a pretty picture. He instead studies the anxieties of young millennials, a generation perennially unsure of the world around them, and distills them into a heartwarming, deferred coming-of-age story. Firstman dreams of who we can become, if only given the chance to grow up in a world where steady ground is hard to find. Peter is growing up alongside his son, hiccups and all.

#6880. One of My Least Favorite Politicians Lost an Election This Week. I Can’t Stop Laughing About It.

This guy was an ur-Trumper before Trump, and I’m so glad we’re rid of him.

This one is for me. Pardon me while I bust a gut.

Kris Kobach of Kansas—and never has a phrase with three K’s been more apt—is one of the founding weasels of the modern voter-suppression effort. If there is an odious policy position concocted since 2008, Kobach has run the laboratory that produced it. Imaginary voter fraud? Kobach led the farcical election-integrity commission during Trump I that fell apart after a contentious 2017 hearing in New Hampshire at which Kobach’s contentions were disemboweled by Maine secretary of state Matthew Dunlap. Draconian immigration policies? As Kansas secretary of state, Kobach tried to initiate a “proof of citizenship” law that lined him up on the wrong side of the League of Women Voters. Outright racism? Kobach told a radio program that the Obama administration was not far from a call for an end to all prosecutions of African Americans. If there was an ur-Trumper before Trump, it was Kris Kobach.

I thought we were rid of him when he ran for governor in 2018 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he ran for the Senate in 2020 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he got tangled up with Steve Bannon’s Build the Wall scam. But he was like the shingles virus in the body politic. He got elected Kansas attorney general in 2022.

And, on Tuesday, and extending into Thursday afternoon, it looks as though Kris Kobach might have lost another election. May it be his last.

#6878. This Breezy Los Angeles House Has “a Little Funk to It”

Designer Mike Moser creates a Santa Monica home that spans decades, styles, and influences—without sacrificing fun.

Above: The living room features a custom red marble coffee table, sofa, and side table by Mike Moser Studio. The wicker chairs are from Matahari; the vintage mirror is from A. Tyner Antiques, and the carpet is from Lawrence of La Brea.

When designer Mike Moser first saw the Santa Monica house, it hadn’t been touched since the ’90s, and no one had lived in it for over a decade. He knew it would require a full gut renovation. But Moser saw that as an opportunity for his clients—UTA’s chief communications officer Cassandra Bujarski, media executive Jason Bergsman, and their daughter—to get exactly the house they wanted. A place that’s at once a little bit Spanish, a tad preppy, and very Californian.

Moser, a Los Angeles-based interior designer who opened his eponymous firm in 2018, met the clients through their real estate broker. The agent, a friend of Moser’s, called him in while the couple was in escrow, asking him to help them imagine what this crumbling house could become with the right eye and guiding hand. They ended up moving forward with the house and hiring Moser to lead the renovation.

Bujarski’s parents were antique and art collectors and she had a vision for the home as a place that would honor that past and help her pass that legacy down to her own child. “Whatever we do,” Moser remembers her saying, “I want it to really feel collected and eclectic.” Other than that guideline, Bujarski and Bergsman gave Moser free rein—almost.

“That was really her only directive,” he says. “She was like, just have fun and play and be colorful and try new things.”

Moser leaned fully into this eclecticism, pulling references that span eras, regions, and styles. He cites 1920s and ’30s Los Angeles houses, Mexican architect Luis Barragán, Milan’s Villa Necchi, and hacienda style all as influences in the house’s design. In the dressing room, Moser added a “menswear, haberdashery, Ralph Lauren feeling,” he says—fitting, as Moser once worked for the brand. He calls this potpourri of inspiration an “amalgamation”: “It’s easy breezy California and we wanted to add a little funk to it.”

Los Angeles is the place to mix and match styles. It’s a city where modern mansions share blocks with Tudors and Craftsman; Spanish Colonials bump up against French Normandy houses. “The beauty of California, especially L.A. architecture, is it's a melting pot,” Moser says. “It's all really about these weird artists and creatives back in the ’30s, ’40s, ’50s that just were marching to the beat of their own drum. So I was like, let's do the same.”

Moser and his team gutted the Spanish-style house and stripped it down to the studs. They had to modernize all the internal systems, bring in new steel and windows, open up the layout to create more “breathing room,” as Moser calls it, and replace the laughing plaster walls. Despite the major work required to make the house livable, Moser still wanted to honor what it used to be. He added Saltillo replica floors and was able to keep the original wooden beams in the living room. In the dining room, he added new wooden beams, calling back to those in the living area, but had artist Nicke Valle paint them with swirling yellow and red spirals.

If the structural elements pay homage to the space’s origins, the furniture is tailored precisely to its new residents. Moser needed to find stylish places to store Bujarski’s collections. “I had an itch to scratch with the casegoods on this project. I was really obsessed with armoires,” the designer says.

There’s a baroque cabinet from Giannetti Home in the entryway, an antique credenza in the dining room, and two vintage Deco Swedish dressers that act as nightstands. “Each one of these cabinets got to be a curio,” Moser says. The dining sideboard houses her china; a vintage Swedish armoire in the family room holds her mother’s Murano candlesticks. In the dining room, the dishware mounted on the wall is vintage Majolica, all with a seafood theme, that Moser and Bujarski hunted down—at auction, on eBay, in antiques stores—in the six months before the install. Throughout the home, “there’s a sense of reveal,” Moser notes.

“I always want to be a chameleon,” Moser adds. “I live as a joyful person. The first thing I say to a client when I take on a project is ‘If this isn't fun, we're doing it wrong.’”

Step Inside This Collected Santa Monica Home

#6875. Iowa Governor-Elect Rob Sand Was Also a Prada Model

On November 3, as part of the blue-vote tsunami, Iowa elected Democratic Governor Rob Sand. The 44 year-old graduated with a political science degree from Brown University and a J.D. degree from University of Iowa College of Law; he even got a Truman scholarship and an honors thesis. And his 2026 campaign was centered on anti-corruption, bipartisanship, affordable living, and public education. But there’s more—he’s also a pretty face.

As it turns out, the governor-elect helped pay for his prestigious college degrees by walking runways in Milan and Paris. At six-feet, three inches, the young, aspiring politician modeled in shows for Giuliano Fujiwara and Number (N)ine. He even made it into a Prada runway, Newsweek reports. It was the early 2000s, and he was reportedly signed to none other than IMG Models—though his profile seems to have been taken down since.

For whatever reason, his modeling past is not something the chiseled governor-elect likes to talk about, the New York Times noted, but it sure makes for quite the resume.

On January 19, 2004, Sand even missed the Iowa caucus in Decorah because he was modeling menswear in Milan. “It had to be one or the other,” he told Newsweek. “And I chose to go to Europe.” (Who wouldn’t?) While Sand did not win that time around, look at him now, years later, up at the top.

Sand took Iowa this week while in a sharp navy-blue suit and a sky-blue checkered button-down that matched his eyes. “Thank you, Iowa! I love you, Iowa!” he excitedly said after catwalking up to the podium for his victory speech.

Rob Sand’s team has not responded to Harper’s Bazaar’s request for comment.

#6873. We’ve Tested Virtually Every At-Home Laser Hair Removal Device—These 9 Stood Out

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

See the best picks of 2026, how they work, the results, and the deals you shouldn't miss.

Here’s my unfiltered review of laser hair removal: It’s painful, expensive, and takes a lot of sessions to see results. Soon, I'm running right back to waxing (ouch) and shaving (annoying). I thought at-home laser hair removal devices would be an expensive little beauty gadget that did nothing—yet another tool piled up next to my abandoned skincare devices. But I was so wrong. They're actually the best of both worlds.

An at-home laser hair removal device actually can help reduce the number of hairs that grow on you by damaging the follicle with every zap—all from your bed, couch, bathroom, wherever. This makes your hair look sparser, creating the illusion that you have far less hair (and making hair removal a whole lot quicker). And yep, at-home laser hair removal devices do work. They just use lower energy levels than the professional devices at a medspa or dermatologist’s office, which means they also require way more treatments to get similar results.

I’ve tried out all the top devices on the market for two months, evaluating how easy they were to use, how long they take on different areas of the body, the level of pain, and the efficacy, all to come up with this list of the top 10 out there. Here’s a peek at our top favorites.

Keep reading for a bit more info on these devices, plus a full review of all of our picks (like how we use them, the results we've seen in two months, and more).

Safety Guidelines and Skin Type Considerations:

Annoyingly, there are currently no at-home laser hair removal devices that are safe to use on dark skin and/or light hair, even if the device claims to work for “everyone.” In reality, they won’t work—or are unsafe for—any skin tones deeper than “light brown” or on hair colors lighter than “medium brown.” So make sure to speak with your dermatologist before wasting your money or, worse, damaging your skin. Got it? Good.
