#6834. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6832. According to Judi Dench, the Biggest James Bond Baddie Was Daniel Craig All Along

It turns out he was behind some of the last few Bond films' most shocking moves.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Daniel Craig’s much-celebrated time as 007 wasn’t just bad news for the world’s supervillains—the good guys dropped like flies, too.

After Craig joined the franchise in 2006’s Casino Royale, the legendary Judi Dench, who had played M since 1995’s GoldenEye, lasted just three films before kicking it. And as we all know—spoiler alert, just in case you haven’t seen the end of Craig’s 007 journey—Bond himself met an explosive end in No Time to Die. It turns out you can thank Craig for both plot twists.

Craig has been vocal about his desire to kill off 007 in the past, but in her new memoir, titled Is It Too Late to Make a Run for It?, Dame Judi Dench shared that he also had a hand in her own character's demise.

"I was taken to the Wolseley restaurant by Barbara [Broccoli], Michael [G. Wilson] and Sam [Mendes]—I don't think Daniel Craig was there—and they outlined the plot and told me they were going to bump me off," she revealed.

But the crucial new information is this: "Apparently, it was Daniel's idea to have me killed," Dame Dench revealed.

Ruthless stuff, but in fairness, it did result in one of the series's most impactful moments. Will the next Bond actor—which, of course, is an extremely hot conversation at the moment—get the same opportunity to decide his co-stars' fates? You would not have thought so. The next M, whoever it may be, can breathe a big sigh of relief.

#6830. A Definitive Guide to the Best Black Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Out of all the fall fashion purchases you make this season, there’s one item that is guaranteed to give you maximum mileage in the months ahead: a pair of black boots.

For those who favor a sleek silhouette to elevate and anchor your outfits, a classic knee-high boot complements dresses and skirts across the board. If you’d like your boots to serve as more of a personality piece, why not test drive a Western-inspired pair, or one detailed with moto hardware? Of course, timeless shapes like square-toe booties and Chelsea boots will always be reliable go-tos; trendier styles like the slouchy boot still feel just as versatile as they did decades ago.

Ahead, we break down nine different black boot styles, from equestrian-inspired riding boots to all-weather lug sole boots. Find the right one for you in our guide to the best black boots of the season.

A quintessential knee-high black boot can be worn layered beneath dresses and skirts or styled over slim trousers and denim. Let your personal preference inform the height—flat or block heels are best for commuters, while a higher heel can transition from the workday to evening seamlessly.

If you’re not yet a Western boot convert, the easiest way to style these silhouettes is to contrast them with wardrobe staples that don’t feel cowboy adjacent. Whether you’re letting your boots peek out from beneath a pair of wool trousers, or opting for a pair with subtler motifs, there’s always a way to make them feel your own.

Saint Laurent Dylan 45 Patent Leather Boots

A traditional Chelsea boot silhouette is the workhorse of many wardrobes. While a classic take on this style makes a great investment, don’t miss modern interpretations, like the Row’s, which are cut extra low.

Manolo Blahnik Chelsa Leather Chelsea Ankle Booties

Whether you’re going for moto-inspired hardware or something with a bit of downtown edge (Coach and Vince, as examples), a silver buckle is the kind of detail that adds dimension while staying versatile.

Manolo Blahnik Calica Buckled Biker Boots

For instant polish, slip into a pair of black riding boots. This equestrian-inspired silhouette is both classic and distinct, ideal with preppy separates like pleated skirts and boxy blazers.

The Row Nobilis Leather Knee-High Riding Boots

Khaite, Chloé, and several other designers included the slouchy boot in their fall 2026 collections, highlighting how this style has shifted away from its ’80s associations and in a much more compelling direction (particularly when rendered in classic black).

Preparing for the winter means being ready for the storms ahead, and a pair of lug-sole boots is a must. The season’s best styles offer all-weather protection (and are a stylish snow-boot alternative), while still feeling versatile enough to wear on glorious days when the weather is agreeable.

Not quite an ankle boot or a knee-high either—the mid-calf boot is shaping up to be one of the season’s most interesting shapes. Need proof? Just look to the designers embracing it—from cool-girl favorite Maryam Nassir Zadeh to buzzy newcomer Jude.

There’s a time and place for a pointed boot, but a sleek square toe always manages to feel a touch cooler. Look for styles with a slimmer, subtler square toe, so you can style them for any occasion.

#6828. How Loyal Is I Play Rocky to Sylvester Stallone’s Real Story?

“This is what he went through,” lead actor Anthony Ippolito tells Esquire.

Before Rocky Balboa climbed the steps of the Philadelphia Museum of Art, and long before he ever came back to defeat Apollo Creed, the iconic boxer was just an idea in Sylvester Stallone’s head. The 30-year-old was basically homeless and struggling to make it as an actor. But he wrote a good script for a moving and honest sports drama—one that he would also reportedly demand to star in or it would never see the light of day.

That’s how much Stallone believed in his work, and now the story of his perseverance has been adapted into a full feature biopic titled I Play Rocky. The film, out in theaters on Friday, November 6, stars Anthony Ippolito (The Offer) as a young Stallone who is desperately trying to get Rocky in the right hands. Once he does, he can tell that the United Artists film studio wants it badly enough to let him play the lead, turning down offers of millions along the way just so he can secure himself a future in Hollywood.

While many biopics fudge the details, most of I Play Rocky is spot-on. He conceived the story after watching a 15-round bout in 1975 between heavyweight champion Muhammad Ali and Chuck Wepner. He turned down massive sums of cash as the studio tried to buy him out from starring in the film, and he was forced to temporarily sell his dog just to afford food for himself. It’s even true that he starred in a softcore porn film, The Party at Kitty and Stud's, where he was paid $200 for two days of work and full-frontal. As Stallone told Playboy in the late ‘70s, “It was either do that movie or rob someone because I was at the very end of my rope.”

If there’s anything I Play Rocky stretches the truth about, it’s that Stallone didn’t write the script in a mad flurry. “I let it incubate for ten months,” he told Forbes in 2017. But the rest—even the scene that shows Stallone punching frozen meat until his fists bleed because the meat hadn’t thawed yet—is all how it really happened.

Plus, Ippolito’s impression of Stallone is one of the best performances we’ve ever seen in a biopic. After seeing the film, Stallone even told Ippolito that it was like looking in a mirror. And as the actor told Esquire last month, his journey to land the role in I Play Rocky wasn’t that different from Stallone’s original pursuit.

“Nobody was asking me to audition for this,” Ippolito said. “I read an article that Peter Farrelly was doing this movie and I just thought, I’d love to try to get in a conversation for that. I sent in a tape that I recorded in my grandmother’s basement, because it was very much stuck in the ’70s and just felt period correct. Then, when I got my hands on the script, I devoured it and sent another tape. You feel sometimes like you’re sending your stuff into an abyss, especially when nobody’s asking for the thing in the first place.”

Eventually, Ippolito was called in and it all took off from there. “I just hope that the film resonates with people. Rocky was so impactful because it’s the underdog story, and this kind of angle is very much true to his real life. This is what he went through, and it’s pretty remarkable.”

#6827. Meet the ‘North of North’ Season 2 Cast and Characters

North of North, the Canadian comedy set in the Arctic Prince of Wales Island, is back on Netflix for season 2. If you’re catching up or in need of a crash course, here’s your guide to the cast & characters in North of North season 2.

The show is about an Inuk woman named Siaja who blows up her life in search of something more nontraditional in terms of gender and sexuality. That’s not easy to do in a small town where her ex-husband was the golden boy. Siaja’s ever-evolving family also shakes things up, especially in season 2 when she meets her half-sister, Olivia, played by Kesha Allurut. As for the cast and characters who’ve been on this journey since season one, here’s who you need to know.

Siaja is a young mother who quits her job and gets a divorce.

Anna Lambe’s previous television credits include the fourth season of True Detective, Three Pines, Alaska Daily, Trickster, and Diggtown. She starred in the film In the Heart of the South and appeared in Heart of the Beast earlier this year. Currently, you can also see Lambe in the film The Social Reckoning.

Prior to North of North, Maika Harper played Anna on Mohawk Girls, Michaela on Kim’s Convenience, and Detective Shina May on Law & Order: Criminal Intent.

Kuuk is Siaja’s love interest, but it’s complicated for a few reasons.

Braeden Clarke won a Canadian Screen Award for playing Leo on Little Bird. Prior to North of North he also appeared on Outlander, SkyMed, and Impulse. His first credit was as a frat guy in Legends of Tomorrow.

Zorga Qaunaq was also Lambe’s co-star in In the Heart of the South. She can be seen next in this year’s feature film In Alaska, which recently debuted at TIFF.

Colin is a local radio host and Siaja’s friend.

Bailey Poching is a comedian whose previous television appearances include the panel shows 7 Days, which he also writes for, as well as Dealbreakers, Sims Logic, and TechTown: Middle Ages.

Jay Ryan was a series regular on Neighbours, Sea Patrol, Go Girls, the 2012 series Beauty & The Beast, Mary Kills People, and Top of the Lake. He also played grown-up Ben in It Chapter Two.

Mary Lynn Rajskub is an actress and comedian best known for playing Chloe on 24 and Gail on It’s Always Sunny in Philadelphia. Other credits include The Dropout, The Girlfriend Experience, @midnight, Californication, and 2 Broke Girls.

Elisapee is another one of Siaja’s coworkers.

Prior to North of North, Doreen Nutaaq Simmonds was an actress and consultant on the fourth season of True Detective.

Fragrance has always been a subtle, almost invisible way to accessorize an outfit. This fall, it’s become something you can actually wear: around your neck, on your finger, or clipped to your bag. #6823. MobLand Season 2, Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

Conrad’s grip on reality is beginning to slip.

Last week I wrote a giddy hagiography of Frankie Cooper. It’s embarrassing how much I like him. I really should grow up, be a man, maintain some critical distance, and write in wry asides like a real English journalist.

But I can’t help it. He’s such an irresistibly murderous bastard that he makes me want to kill a cat just to see how it feels. (The lawyers have advised me to clarify that I don’t actually want to kill a cat; no cats were harmed in the making of this opinion.)

Well, Frankie, it’s been lovely. But there’s a new psychopath in town. Of course the Medusa had a vengeful son by the name of “Mad Dog”! Just out of prison for murder, he’s seen CCTV footage of Eddie, who was there when his daddy was killed, and he’s coming to the Cotswolds.

“I’ll find them, Daddy,” says Kelly, pouring a bottle of whisky over his father’s coffin.

But all that’s for episode 9. We’ve got the admin of episode 8 to sort through first.

Remember Harry’s words at the end of episode 7? “Is that Michael Cooper? At 3 a.m. you’re gonna get clapped.”

Well, Harry did indeed have Frankie Cooper’s boy killed. But not quite so easily as we were first led to believe. He wanted to fuck with Frankie in the process. Harry knew that Michael would call his dad, who has Newgate Prison sewn up tighter than a dodgy tailor’s pocket. Frankie therefore arranged for Michael to be moved to the solitary wing, where he would be safe.

But as Harry explains to Kevin: “What Frankie’s forgot is that a bent screw is a bent screw… So if the CM’s [custodial manager] gonna take a tenner, what’s he gonna do for a twenty? Or in this case, 200 grand in a black bag in the dead of night on a pissing-down Thursday.”

Harry then pays a couple of hired goons to string Michael up in his cell. Frankie is not going to be happy.

It’s time we gave Emmett J. Scanlan his flowers . He’s been ticking over in the background, doing a solid, likable job as the Harrigan dogsbody Paul. But he is changing now and emerging like a butterfly into the show’s comic sideline. There’s a real touch of the Baldricks about him, with a stroke of Hamlet’s gravedigger—the loyal dogsbody who gets lumbered with the shitty jobs and stays funny in spite of it all.

The poor sod thinks he’s in for the noose after the Sicily debacle of episode 7. He’s begging for his life, so desperate that he thinks VIP tickets to Rod Stewart might save him. But no. They were never going to kill Paul. It was merely a simple lampoon. They want him to cut up Donnie’s stinking body in a shipping container.

So Paul strips down to his leopard-print pants and, swinging his hips to the Rolling Stones’ “Mr Charm,” sets about chopping Donnie into vacuum-packable “bricks.” It’s all very Tom Cruise in Risky Business, but with more blood and less hairbrush. Long live Paul.

Eddie’s sinking deeper into the soup—and it’s all of his own doing. If only he weren’t—in Kevin’s words—such a “lazy, spoilt, entitled little cunt.” Maeve finds him in the sauna. He tells her their “situation” isn’t going to work.

“Maeve, I fear our relationship is sailing into choppy water. You’re 80. I’m barely legal.”

Maeve feigns surprise. “This approach, this ploy. It’s depraved. And deeply optimistic.”

Conrad, Eddie explains, is going to “piss his kegs.” Suddenly, so is Eddie, but not out of laughter. “Please don’t tell him; I’ll do anything.”

She agrees, but only if Eddie apologizes. On his knees. To her phone camera. It’s all fun and games for Maeve. I suspect we haven’t seen the last of that video.

Jan agrees to meet Harry. She wants to come clean, to pour out her heart. She tries to explain that she had a brief affair with a man who was using her to get to Harry and Conrad. But Harry already knows.

“That’s my job,” he tells her. “To anticipate danger. Control outcomes. Minimize unwanted consequences.”

She asks him about killing. About the nightmares. He says he doesn’t have any. You get the feeling Harry is all out of nightmares. Then he offers to help Jan and Gina run. Forever.

“I can make that happen. Tonight,” he tells her. “You never have to see me again.”

“What about you?” she asks. “Does no part of you want that?”

For a moment, Harry almost seems human. Almost.

It’s clear now: Conrad is losing his marbles. He wanders into the woods, calling out for his dead dogs. Then he stops, surrounded by swirling mists and squawking crows, and seems to realize just how far he’s slipping. He summons Kevin to the Cotswolds, to which the latter reluctantly submits. Conrad tells his son he’s seen a doctor. He wants to talk succession plans. Kevin, astonished at being asked such a question, reels furiously through the options.

First, Eddie: “A lazy, entitled psychopath who I wouldn’t trust to light the barbecue.”

Then Seraphina: “She would make a world-class businesswoman, but a kingpin? I don’t think she’s ever even touched a gun.”

And then, just as Kevin is about to pitch himself, Conrad reveals what’s really going on. “I see the dead,” he hisses with a haunted, hounded look, the air of the ancient mariner. “They come to me at night. I see them in the woods. All the bodies, in the clothes they died in, their wounds fresh. Standing there, staring at me. They’re in my head, they’re in my soul.”

It’s all very King Lear. They embrace and it’s lovely.

Kevin goes to get the old man a brandy to calm his nerves. When he returns, the fear is gone from Conrad’s eyes. In its place: pure steel. And a shotgun pointed at Kevin’s head.

“If you breathe a word of this to Bella, I’ll kill you both,” he growls.

Conrad is another man again. As though the whole confession were just a performance. But it wasn’t. It was a glimpse of the terrifying disintegration of a man who can no longer distinguish between his different selves.

Kevin can’t believe it. “Pull the trigger then,” he shouts snottily.

“Fuck off now, Kevin,” Conrad says. And, like a beaten dog (though of course, Conrad would never treat a dog in this way), off Kevin fucks.

Well, we’ve spent most of this episode wondering about Frankie’s response to the murder of his son. But here we are. Harry calls Frankie. He wants to reach a treaty. He’s killed Frankie’s son, and now he hopes Frankie will back down.

Here we see a side to Frankie we haven’t seen before: crying, blubbering, a human man in real pain. He’s looking through old photographs of Michael.

“He’s dead, Harry,” he says through snot and tears. “Why did you have to do that? I can’t explain it. The pain.”

“It’s the game,” says Harry, heavy as stone.

To which Frankie replies: “Anyway, I’m sure you understand. After all, Harry, you’re a father too. Ain’tcha?”

It’s a less-than-veiled threat; Frankie’s message: this war isn’t over until the babies are all dead. He’s going after Gina.

And so we end the episode in truly miserable style. A montage of sadness, soundtracked by Johnny Cash’s cover of “Ain’t No Grave”:

The Harrigans have made a lot of enemies over the past eighteen episodes. And now, at last, the dead are joining the queue.

#6822. So...What’s Going on With the Bachelor Franchise? We Finally Have a Premiere Date for ‘The Bachelor’ Season 30

Ever since ABC pulled the plug on Taylor Frankie Paul’s season of The Bachelorette just days before its premiere, fans have been wondering: What’s next for the Bachelor franchise? And after a month of rumors surrounding the fate of The Bachelor and its many spin-offs, fans finally have an actual update. According to Variety, The Bachelor season 30 is set to premiere on Sunday, March 21, 2027.

It was first revealed earlier this year by Deadline that both The Bachelor and Bachelor in Paradise would return for new seasons in 2027.

“The Bachelor is almost a perfect format and it is really powerful,” Disney’s reality chief Rob Mills told the outlet at the time. “It’s over two decades old, and it’s seen really good days and really bad days. But it’s always had a lot of resilience, especially when the people making it really, really care about it, because that fan base also really cares about it. However The Bachelor comes back next year, it will be done with a lot of thoughtfulness and care. I do think better days are ahead for the franchise.”

As a reminder, season 22 of The Bachelorette was scheduled to premiere on March 22 but was canceled amid reports that Taylor and her ex, Dakota Mortensen, were involved in a domestic dispute.

A statement from a Disney Entertainment Television spokesperson was released on March 19, saying, “In light of the newly released video just surfaced today, we have made the decision to not move forward with the new season of The Bachelorette at this time, and our focus is on supporting the family.”

Rumors about how the franchise would move forward (if at all) have been circulating ever since, as the decision to cancel Taylor’s season reportedly also impacted production on The Bachelor and Bachelor in Paradise. TMZ previously reported that the network had planned to cast one of the finalists on Taylor’s season as the next Bachelor but “it was all predicated on that contender becoming famous” from Taylor’s season…which, of course, didn’t happen.

Likewise, a separate source told TMZ that the season 22 contestants would possibly have another chance at love on the upcoming season of Bachelor in Paradise, though “it’s unclear how many might get that opportunity.”

And according to yet another source for People, Warner Bros. reportedly held a meeting with Taylor’s men on the same day the show was canceled (save for her winner, Doug Mason) and told them their “contracts will be in place for a year.”

As for Taylor’s unaired season of The Bachelorette? Rob told THR that he doesn’t know whether it will ever air on ABC or stream on Hulu and is taking everything “a day at a time”...but at this point, it feels safe to conclude that season 22 of The Bachelorette will be shelved indefinitely.

#6820. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6819. Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian’s NYC Night Out Adds to Their Quirky 11-Year History

Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian enjoyed a night out together in New York City yesterday, beginning the evening in regular attire and then ending it in matching Skims pajamas and robes as they left the Four Seasons hotel. At first glance, the pairing might seem unexpected, but Lawrence’s history with the Kardashian family goes back for over 10 years.

In 2023, Lawrence told Kylie Jenner while interviewing her for Interview magazine that she started following the family’s original reality show Keeping Up With the Kardashians when she was a teenager: “I’ve been watching the show since I was, not to date myself, 14, so I would say I know everything about your family,” she shared.

Her first big public moment with the Kardashian-Jenner family was in 2015. Kardashian’s mother, Kris Jenner, posted a photo of Lawrence in bed with her along with a birthday message.

In November 2017, Lawrence interviewed Kardashian on Jimmy Kimmel Live! while serving as a guest host for Jimmy Kimmel.

Kardashian revealed where they first met. “We were at a hotel in New York, and I don’t know where we were coming from, but it was like, 8, and we were going up to see this Axel Vervoordt suite at the Greenwich [Hotel] and I’m getting in the elevator with Kanye [West] and the doors are about to close and I hear Jennifer screaming, ‘I love your show!’ so loud across the whole lobby,” she recalled.

They also discussed Lawrence having dinner at Kris’s house two weeks before their interview and Kardashian dressing her up.

It marked their first significant public conversation:

Less than a year later, Lawrence quipped during a February 2018 event at The Wing that her friendship with Kardashian doesn’t go both ways. “I don’t know that she’d call me her friend,” Lawrence said. “It’s probably a one-sided friendship.”

She recalled learning she’d interview Kardashian on Kimmel’s show. “When I found out that I was going to be interviewing her, I was like, ‘Oh my God, my whole life has been building up to this moment,’” she said. “I wanted to get as much information as I could. I wanted to do my due diligence on Kim Kardashian. I went over to Kris [Jenner’s] house and had dinner with the whole family.”

Of the family generally, Lawrence said, “They are smart, grounded and normal and funny. They’re very nice people, [they’re] very close, [they have a] loyal bond with one another, which I think is a positive thing to put out into the world. They’ve been absolutely lovely to me.”

Lawrence has remained close to the Kardashian-Jenner family in the years since. (Kris even attended Lawrence’s wedding to Cooke Maroney in October 2019.)

During her 2023 conversation with Kylie, Lawrence even jokingly referred to Kris as her mother while asking a question about Kylie’s relationship with her older sister Kendall Jenner. “You and me are Leos,” she told Kylie. “We’re happy, excited golden retrievers. And Kendall, of course, is a Scorpio like our mother, Kris.” She and Kylie ended their chat by exchanging I love yous.

Last month, Lawrence was mentioned on The Kardashians, with Kardashian revealing the actress had asked for Kourtney Kardashian’s contact information to say sorry for a remark she made during a Vanity Fair interview about Kourtney.

Kardashian said that Lawrence wrote in her apology email, “I just feel like I’m one of your sisters.”

#6818. The Black Doves Season 2 Finale Has an Insane Cliffhanger

Let’s talk about Helen Webb and what it all means for season 3.

After a two-year wait, Netflix's Christmas crime caper Black Doves is finally back for its second outing. It's a messy delight that tests the loyalties of the central characters in ways the first season never dared and—both annoyingly and excitingly—ends on just as agonizing a cliffhanger.

In the first season, Elizabeth Reed (Sarah Lancashire) was something of a mystery: an ominous, witty controller of sorts who kept Helen Webb (Keira Knightley) and Sam Young (Ben Whishaw) on their toes. But this time around, Black Doves spends some time unravelling her past, as well as that of the titular intelligence agency: a backstory that informs and explains the major threat of the season, the German anarchist organization Kestrel.

Though the secrets reveal themselves slowly, a bombshell-filled (pun very much intended) finale crashes through a series of twists that—if you didn't see them coming (which I can't say I did, for some of them)—will really feel like a stab in the face. No, not the back, because unfortunately the answers were always lying right in front of us.

There's a lot to break down from that last episode, so read on for our recap of Black Doves season 2 and an explanation of the ending—including that twist that'll probably have a drastic impact on where we'll be at the beginning of season 3.

The penultimate episode ended with a bunch of crossed wires, conflicting contracts, and potential deaths. Eleanor and Williams have taken out paid hits on Laila and Sam, while Sam's final target in his prison-break contract with Kestrel is none other than Reed (after episode 4 revealed that she is in fact the mysterious “Wildflower” of the files she tried to take from Alexi Chernov).

The main action kicks off when the precious international peace summit that Wallace (Andrew Buchan) has spent the whole season planning gets upended before it even starts. Wallace and Helen's cab is intercepted and chased, while U. S. defense envoy Cecile Mason (Neve Campbell) and recently widowed Russian envoy Katia Chernov (Sylvia Hoeks) are captured by strange armed men.

When Patrick (Sam Riley), the Kestrel man who's been blackmailing Sam all season, escorts Reed back into the Black Doves office with a gun to her head, it quickly becomes clear that 'Wildflower', or Reed, is the only target Kestrel wants brought in alive. Or, at least, whoever's running it does.

And there's the twist: after a horrid little hostage situation in the office results in the deaths of quite a few senior Black Doves and Mason, Katia reveals that she is in fact the head of Kestrel and, in turn, Vita—the long-lost, abandoned daughter that Reed left in Berlin after killing her fake husband and running off (ouch).

Dani, the nuisance of a secretary Reed planted in Wallace's office to seduce him and potentially replace Helen, has ended up being recruited by Kestrel. But she doesn't last long after a game of grenade hot potato played by Eleanor, Williams, and Laila ends with Dani being caught in a lift with it. Even Reed's capable of mistakes.

Meanwhile, over in the car park where Helen and Wallace end up after fleeing, Mr. Prime Minister finally decides to question his wife about her past and her secrets. Obviously, she lies again—until the situation shifts a little.

Helen receives a series of ominous messages and threatening instructions, first over text, then through a discreet earpiece planted in her car. Katia, whom Helen very quickly realizes is not an ally, instructs her to kill Wallace and turn herself in to MI5. But what's the leverage, you ask?

Marie, the housekeeper who was teased as a Black Dove at the end of the first season, has in fact been working for Kestrel this whole time. If you're a babysitter to the Prime Minister's spy wife, who's been planted by a capitalist intelligence agency, but you're working for a German anarchist organization, does that make you a double or a triple agent? A nightmare for Mrs Webb, more than anything.

Helen tricks Katia over the phone by shooting past Wallace towards the window, explains (on a surface level) that the children are in danger and she needs to hijack the car, and darts off with Sam to rescue the children from Marie.

After confronting Marie, Helen shoots her in the head and flees the scene, while Reed reconciles (not quite) with her bleeding-out daughter in her last moments. Kestrel's down and all is well? Obviously not, because it's never that simple.

MI5 is even more suspicious of Helen than ever before, but Wallace is covering for her. Conteh, as one of the few surviving senior members of the Black Doves, restores Reed to her former standing.

Helen and Sam reconcile properly after their heated argument during the first Reed rescue mission of the season, and he reveals that he's going to leave the country once again. After they part, a strange man bumps into Helen and, BOOM, final twist! Patrick has escaped in perfect condition and Kestrel is still going strong. He leaves Helen with his card, more as a threat than anything else.

Reed and Helen agree to increase Helen's standing and role within the Black Doves, meaning that she'll be more than just an intel-grabber going forward, and putting her in a pretty good spot work-wise. However, things aren't quite so simple at home.

Wallace confronts Helen once again about her real name (Lily Morris), and the finale ends with what appears to be the beginning of a conversation about her past. But how much is Helen actually going to tell Wallace? There's only so much she can give away without completely compromising her position, but she'll have to say enough to regain his trust. We'll find out in two years, I suppose.
